WARNING — \"per-layer granularity\"**：已在 Round 1 修正中完成\n- grep 确认：零匹配\n- related_work 末句已删除 per-layer claim\n- discussion §5.4 已删除 per-layer claim\n\n### Round 3 新增内容（相对 Round 2）\n\n**Limitations \"Repetition confound\" 段扩充**（Director CRITICAL 2 补充指令）：\n- 原文仅 2 句（acknowledge confound + absent at α≤0.5）\n- 扩充为 3 句，增加缓解因素：α=0.5 的 2,458 real + 1,500 synth 低重复率、dose-response 仍成立、α=0 control 排除训练过程本身的混淆\n\n### 审查重点\n\n1. **Scope claims 一致性**：全文是否统一为 \"three configurations / 0.5B+1.5B / WikiText-2\"\n2. **术语一致性**：无 \"weight-space\"、无 \"per-layer granularity\" overclaim\n3. **CRITICAL 2 完整性**：Method §3.1 上采样说明 + Limitations repetition confound（含缓解因素）是否充分\n4. **数字一致性**：Abstract/Intro/Experiments/Discussion/Conclusion 中的关键数字（d=33.48, AUROC=0.984 等）\n5. **页数**：主正文 8 页\n\n### 修改后的关键内容\n\n**Limitations \"Repetition confound\"（扩充后）:**\n```latex\n\\paragraph{Repetition confound.}\nAt $\\alpha = 1.0$, the 1{,}500 unique synthetic chunks are upsampled to 4{,}916, introducing repetition (${\\sim}3.3\\times$ per epoch) that may contribute to observed training dynamics through memorization rather than synthetic data quality degradation alone.\nHowever, this confound is mitigated at moderate contamination: at $\\alpha = 0.5$, the corpus contains 2{,}458 real and 1{,}500 synthetic chunks with minimal repetition, yet the dose-response trend and partial recovery conclusions (Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy}) remain intact.\nThe $\\alpha = 0$ control undergoes the identical training procedure without collapse, ruling out the training process itself as a confound.\n```\n\n**Method §3.1 Mix（上采样说明，Round 2 已有）:**\n```latex\n    When the number of synthetic chunks is fewer than $|\\mathcal{D}_\\text{real}|$, we upsample with replacement to maintain a fixed training set size of $|\\mathcal{D}_\\text{real}|$ across all configurations.\n```",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Y3aLVtdxon8WUQ2KoCrwd2`</summary>

```text
41	\subsection{Monitoring Signals}
42	\label{sec:signals}
43	
44	We monitor four families of training-time signals (seven signals total), collected every 50 optimizer steps.
45	Spectral signals and evaluation perplexity are computed on a held-out validation set in evaluation mode; train loss is logged from the training set by the Trainer.
46	
47	\paragraph{Spectral signals.}
48	Given $n$ validation sequences, we collect last-layer hidden states into a matrix $\mathbf{H} \in \mathbb{R}^{T \times d}$ ($T$ total tokens, $d$ hidden dimension), center by subtracting the column mean, and compute the sample covariance $\mathbf{C} = \frac{1}{T-1}\mathbf{H}^\top\mathbf{H}$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_1 \geq \cdots \geq \lambda_d \geq 0$.
49	
50	\emph{Stable rank} measures the effective dimensionality of the representation:
51	\begin{equation}
52	    \mathrm{SR}(\mathbf{C}) = \frac{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d} \lambda_i\right)^2}{\sum_{i=1}^{d} \lambda_i^2}.
53	    \label{eq:stable_rank}
54	\end{equation}
55	Under model collapse, $\mathrm{SR}$ \emph{increases}: the Frobenius norm $\|\mathbf{H}\|_F^2$ grows faster than the leading eigenvalue $\lambda_1$, dispersing energy across more dimensions rather than concentrating it.%
56	\footnote{This is the participation ratio of the covariance eigenspectrum, closely related to but distinct from the matrix stable rank $\|\mathbf{H}\|_F^2 / \|\mathbf{H}\|_2^2$; the two are monotonically related for centered data.}
57	
58	\emph{Spectral entropy} provides a complementary measure of spectral concentration:
59	\begin{equation}
60	    \mathrm{SE}(\mathbf{C}) = \frac{-\sum_{i=1}^{d} p_i \log p_i}{\log d}, \quad p_i = \frac{\lambda_i}{\sum_{j=1}^{d} \lambda_j}.
61	    \label{eq:spectral_entropy}
62	\end{equation}
63	$\mathrm{SE} \in [0,1]$, with $\mathrm{SE}=1$ for a uniform spectrum and $\mathrm{SE} \to 0$ for concentration on few components.
64	We use $n=64$ validation chunks for SVD computation.
65	
66	\paragraph{Output signals.}
67	We monitor three output-level signals computed on different data splits.
68	
69	\emph{Train loss} $\mathcal{L}_\text{train}$ is the per-batch cross-entropy on the current training corpus, logged every 50 optimizer steps by the HuggingFace Trainer.
70	Because contaminated data has lower entropy than real text, train loss \emph{decreases} under collapse---the model fits synthetic output easily.
71	This makes train loss an effective within-generation detector of data distribution change, though a decrease in train loss does not imply improved model quality.
72	
73	\emph{Evaluation perplexity} is computed on a held-out clean validation set:
74	\begin{equation}
75	    \mathrm{PPL}_\text{eval} = \exp\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{V}|} \ell(\mathbf{x}_i)\right),
76	    \label{eq:perplexity}
77	\end{equation}
78	where $\ell(\mathbf{x}_i)$ is the cross-entropy loss on validation sequence $\mathbf{x}_i$ and $N$ is the total token count.
79	Evaluation perplexity tracks degradation in language modeling quality on real text across generations, but its within-generation utility is limited (Section~\ref{sec:early_warning_results}).
80	Note that training perplexity $\mathrm{PPL}_\text{train} = \exp(\mathcal{L}_\text{train})$ is a monotone transform of train loss; we report train loss directly since it is the quantity logged during training.
81	
82	\emph{Token entropy} measures the mean per-position entropy of the next-token distribution:
83	\begin{equation}
84	    \mathrm{TE}(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{L-1}\sum_{t=1}^{L-1} H\!\left(P_\theta(\cdot \mid x_{\leq t})\right), \quad H(P) = -\sum_v P(v) \log P(v),
85	    \label{eq:token_entropy}
86	\end{equation}
87	averaged over 100 validation chunks.
88	Decreasing token entropy indicates the model is becoming more peaked in its predictions, a hallmark of mode collapse in generation.
89	
90	\paragraph{Gradient signal.}
91	We log the L2 gradient norm $\|\nabla_\theta \mathcal{L}\|_2$ before each optimizer step.
92	Under QLoRA, gradient norm exhibits non-monotonic behavior because gradients are confined to a rank-$r$ adapter subspace~\citep{hu2022lora} with NF4 quantization noise~\citep{dettmers2023qlora}; Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy} analyzes this method-dependence.
93	
94	\paragraph{Downstream signal.}
95	We evaluate HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag} accuracy (4-choice commonsense reasoning, 1{,}000 samples) at the end of each generation as a downstream task probe.
```

```text
1	% Abstract — collapse_severity_monitor
2	% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B
3	
4	\begin{abstract}
5	Iterative training on model-generated data causes model collapse---progressive loss of distribution tails, contraction of output diversity, and degradation of acquired knowledge---yet the metrics practitioners most commonly watch during training fail to detect it.
6	We systematically evaluate four families of training-time signals---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (train loss, evaluation perplexity, token entropy), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---as collapse severity monitors across three experimental configurations spanning two model scales (0.5B, 1.5B), two fine-tuning methods (QLoRA, full fine-tuning), and six contamination fractions ($\alpha \in \{0, 0.1, \ldots, 1.0\}$) on WikiText-2.
7	We find that different signal families have complementary operating ranges.
8	Spectral entropy achieves the largest cross-generation effect size (Cohen's $d = 33.48$, $\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$ across seeds), while stable rank provides the strongest within-generation early warning ($\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.94$ at 10\% training progress, $0.984$ pooled, 34.4\% deviation at 3\% of training).
9	Held-out evaluation perplexity provides no within-generation early warning (pooled $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.533$, indistinguishable from random), while train loss is an effective but interpretively ambiguous detector (pooled $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$): its decrease reflects successful fitting of corrupted data, not improved model quality.
10	The same signal can be informative under one training method and misleading under another: gradient norm is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning.
11	These findings yield a three-tier monitoring strategy---stable rank for within-generation alarms, evaluation perplexity for cross-generation tracking, spectral entropy for severity discrimination---and a signal taxonomy characterizing where each family succeeds, saturates, or misleads.
12	\end{abstract}
13	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WSXCQgCQiMK8xQnE3kdK8k`</summary>

```text
8:Section~\ref{sec:correlation} establishes that spectral and output signals track collapse severity across configurations.
9:Section~\ref{sec:early_warning_results} shows that certain signals detect collapse within a single training run before evaluation perplexity diverges.
10:Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy} reveals that signal informativeness depends on the fine-tuning method, motivating a taxonomy of method-dependent operating ranges.
15:We quantify signal sensitivity using Cohen's $d$ between the clean baseline ($\alpha = 0$) and full contamination ($\alpha = 1.0$) at generation~4, computed across three seeds from the C2 configuration (Qwen2.5-1.5B, WikiText-2; interim 3 of 5 planned seeds; Table~\ref{tab:configs}).
20:Distinct-1's large endpoint separation coexists with non-monotonic intermediate behavior at 0.5B (Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
22:Token entropy, self-BLEU, gradient norm, and HellaSwag accuracy yield small effect sizes ($d < 5$), placing them in a second tier for cross-generation severity discrimination (Figure~\ref{fig:severity_curves}).
31:Six of seven monitored signals maintain consistent direction of change under both QLoRA and full fine-tuning (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}): spectral entropy, stable rank, evaluation perplexity, token entropy, distinct-1, and self-BLEU all move in the same direction as contamination increases, regardless of training method.
32:The exception is gradient norm, which exhibits non-monotonic behavior under QLoRA but monotonic decrease under full fine-tuning (Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
43:Cross-generation tracking (Section~\ref{sec:correlation}) requires completing multiple generations to assess severity.
45:We evaluate this using the relative deviation $\Delta_s$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:deviation}) and AUROC for binary collapse detection at each checkpoint.
48:Stable rank achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.94$ at 10\% training progress for distinguishing $\alpha = 1.0$ from $\alpha = 0$, reaching $0.984$ over the full run (Figure~\ref{fig:roc_curves}; Table~\ref{tab:within_gen_roc}).
49:At step~50 (3\% of training), $\Delta_\mathrm{SR} = 34.4\%$---far exceeding the 5\% detection threshold defined in Section~\ref{sec:evaluation}.
50:Stable rank's within-generation sensitivity reflects the structural nature of spectral signals: contamination alters the covariance eigenspectrum before output-level metrics respond, because energy dispersal across representation dimensions (Eq.~\ref{eq:stable_rank}) is detectable even when the model's token-level predictions have not yet degraded.
59:However, its AUROC trajectory reveals early-training direction reversal (Figure~\ref{fig:roc_curves}b): $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.24$ at 10\% progress, rising to $0.48$ at 20\%, $0.85$ at 50\%, and $0.923$ at completion.
67:This failure has a structural explanation: within a single generation, the model trains on a fixed corpus $\mathcal{D}_g$ and has not yet produced the synthetic data that will contaminate $\mathcal{D}_{g+1}$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:mixing}).
80:The cross-method comparison (C1 vs C4; Table~\ref{tab:configs}) reveals that the same signal can be informative under one training method and misleading under another.
86:This method-dependence means gradient norm cannot be used as a collapse indicator without knowing the training configuration (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}).
95:Token entropy captures this concentration (Section~\ref{sec:correlation}), but $n$-gram diversity metrics do not.
99:Full fine-tuning produces sharper onset but lower terminal severity than QLoRA (Figure~\ref{fig:method_comparison}).
102:This generation~2 dip echoes the partial recovery observed at moderate contamination (Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy}), suggesting that full parameter access allows transient re-adaptation even under full synthetic pressure.
116:Based on the evidence above, we propose signal-to-task assignments (Table~\ref{tab:signal_taxonomy}):
127:Effect sizes in Section~\ref{sec:correlation} are from C2 (1.5B, $N=3$ interim seeds); within-generation AUROC values are from C1 (0.5B, $N=1$ seed); method-dependent operating ranges are from C1 vs C4 (0.5B, $N=1$ seed each).
```

The full fine-tuning baseline shows a PPL ratio of about 16.96, which rounds to 17×, but I need to confirm whether this is actually from the C4 dataset context or if it's being cited in the wrong section—the paragraph appears in the QLoRA 0.5B discussion, where the actual collapse ratio would be much higher at around 53×. So the 53.5× ratio comes from the QLoRA 0.5B data, but that doesn't match the 17× mentioned in the text. The 17× actually corresponds to the full FT results (326.19/19.23), which suggests there might be a mismatch between where that figure is cited and which model configuration it actually refers to. So the 17× multiplier does come from the full fine-tuning data at generation 4. But I'm second-guessing whether the self-BLEU discussion is actually referring to that terminal value or to some intermediate generation in the QLoRA results where the ratio might also reach around 17×.

Looking at the actual numbers from full FT, 326.19 divided by 19.23 gives exactly 17.0×, which is a clean match. I'll annotate this as generation 4 with α = 1.0 on C4. Now I need to finalize these edits and move forward, then check for any same-section self-references like the one in experiments.tex at line 102.

There's a cross-subsection reference at line 95 pointing back within the same results section that might draw reviewer attention, but the real issue is line 102 where "Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy}" references the current subsection itself—that's a true self-reference that needs to change to "below".

For the forward references at lines 20 and 32, those are pointing to a later subsection so I'll leave them as is. Now I'm compiling my full list of edits: the method.tex changes for families and count, adding the diversity paragraph, updating the abstract with diversity family and α notation, the footnote for distinct-1 in experiments.tex, and fixing the "Six of seven" phrasing.

    "newString": "We systematically evaluate five families of training-time signals---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (train loss, evaluation perplexity, token entropy), diversity (distinct-$n$, self-BLEU), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---as collapse severity monitors across three experimental configurations spanning two model scales (0.5B, 1.5B), two fine-tuning methods (QLoRA, full fine-tuning), and six contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0\\}$) on WikiText-2.",
    "originalFile": "% Abstract — collapse_severity_monitor\n% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nIterative training on model-generated data causes model collapse---progressive loss of distribution tails, contraction of output diversity, and degradation of acquired knowledge---yet the metrics practitioners most commonly watch during training fail to detect it.\nWe systematically evaluate four families of training-time signals---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (train loss, evaluation perplexity, token entropy), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---as collapse severity monitors across three experimental configurations spanning two model scales (0.5B, 1.5B), two fine-tuning methods (QLoRA, full fine-tuning), and six contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, \\ldots, 1.0\\}$) on WikiText-2.\nWe find that different signal families have complementary operating ranges.\nSpectral entropy achieves the largest cross-generation effect size (Cohen's $d = 33.48$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$ across seeds), while stable rank provides the strongest within-generation early warning ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.94$ at 10\\% training progress, $0.984$ pooled, 34.4\\% deviation at 3\\% of training).\nHeld-out evaluation perplexity provides no within-generation early warning (pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.533$, indistinguishable from random), while train loss is an effective but interpretively ambiguous detector (pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$): its decrease reflects successful fitting of corrupted data, not improved model quality.\nThe same signal can be informative under one training method and misleading under another: gradient norm is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning.\nThese findings yield a three-tier monitoring strategy---stable rank for within-generation alarms, evaluation perplexity for cross-generation tracking, spectral entropy for severity discrimination---and a signal taxonomy characterizing where each family succeeds, saturates, or misleads.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

    "originalFile": "% Introduction — collapse_severity_monitor\n% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nAs LLM-generated text pervades web-scraped training corpora, practitioners increasingly fine-tune models on data shaped by prior model generations.\nThis recursive dynamic creates self-consuming loops that cause \\emph{model collapse}---progressive loss of distribution tails, contraction of output diversity, and degradation of acquired knowledge~\\citep{Shumailov2024,Alemohammad2024}.\nThe problem compounds: even trace synthetic contamination induces collapse at sufficient depth~\\citep{Dohmatob2025}, with errors accumulating across iterations at accelerating rates~\\citep{Dohmatob2024_tails,Fu2024_icml}.\nYet the metric practitioners most commonly rely on for quality assessment---held-out evaluation perplexity---fails to detect this degradation within a run, achieving pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.533$ for within-generation collapse detection (indistinguishable from random chance).\nTrain loss does change under collapse (pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$), but its decrease is interpretively ambiguous: the model fits lower-entropy synthetic data more easily, which looks like healthy convergence.\nA practitioner watching only perplexity or interpreting train loss at face value would have no reliable warning that collapse is underway.\n\nExisting work addresses model collapse from two directions.\nOn the theoretical side, \\citet{Shumailov2024} established the phenomenon, \\citet{Dohmatob2025} proved that any nonzero synthetic fraction eventually induces strong collapse, and \\citet{Dohmatob2024_tails} showed that collapse alters neural scaling laws.\nOn the mitigation side, data accumulation~\\citep{Gerstgrasser2024}, loss modification~\\citep{Zibakhsh2025}, and verification-based filtering~\\citep{Feng2025} each reduce collapse pressure under specific conditions.\nMissing from this landscape is a systematic analysis of which \\emph{training-time signals} best track collapse severity, when they provide early warning, and where they saturate or actively mislead.\n\\citet{Schaeffer2025} highlight that even the definition of collapse varies across studies, further motivating signal-level analysis grounded in controlled experiments.\nThe two closest concurrent works---SIGMA~\\citep{Gu2026}, which monitors output embedding spectra, and the topological index of \\citet{Kalinowski2026}---each address part of this gap but carry limitations: SIGMA requires a clean reference distribution and operates post-hoc; topological monitoring incurs higher computational cost.\nNeither provides a cross-family comparison of signal operating ranges or quantitative within-generation early warning thresholds.\n\nWe conduct a systematic empirical study of training-time signals as collapse severity monitors.\nWe evaluate four signal families---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (train loss, evaluation perplexity, token entropy), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---across three experimental configurations spanning two model scales (0.5B, 1.5B), two fine-tuning methods (QLoRA and full fine-tuning), and six contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0\\}$) on WikiText-2.\nOur central finding is that different signal families have complementary operating ranges, and the same signal's utility can flip from informative to misleading depending on the training method.\n\nOur contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Signal--severity correlation.}\n    Spectral and output signals track collapse across contamination levels.\n    Spectral entropy achieves the largest effect size (Cohen's $d = 33.48$, coefficient of variation $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$ across seeds), followed by evaluation perplexity ($d = 20.82$) and stable rank ($d = 9.43$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$).\n    Validated across QLoRA and full fine-tuning, six of seven monitored signals maintain consistent direction of change regardless of training method.\n    The exception---gradient norm, which is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning---demonstrates that method-dependence is a first-order concern for monitoring pipeline design.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Within-generation early warning and negative results.}\n    Stable rank achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.94$ at 10\\% training progress ($0.984$ pooled) for binary collapse detection, with 34.4\\% deviation from baseline at 3\\% of training.\n    Train loss provides the second-strongest within-generation signal (pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$), though its decrease reflects successful fitting of corrupted data rather than improved model quality.\n    Held-out evaluation perplexity achieves pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.533$ for within-generation detection---the most commonly relied-upon quality metric fails at the most actionable task---and token entropy achieves pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$, providing no reliable discrimination despite a large initial deviation.\n    Evaluation perplexity's failure has a structural explanation: it accumulates degradation through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single generation's training.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Signal taxonomy with method-dependent operating ranges.}\n    Signal informativeness depends on the fine-tuning method and model scale.\n    Gradient norm is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning.\n    HellaSwag accuracy is insensitive at 0.5B scale (relative change ${<}3\\%$, $N{=}1$).\n    Distinct-1 is non-monotonic, reversing direction between moderate and full contamination.\n    Full fine-tuning produces sharper collapse onset ($15.3\\times$ evaluation perplexity ratio at generation~1) than QLoRA ($8.7\\times$) but lower terminal severity.\n    These findings motivate a three-tier recommendation: stable rank for within-generation alarms, evaluation perplexity for cross-generation tracking, and spectral entropy for severity discrimination.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:related_work} surveys collapse theory, spectral analysis of neural networks, and concurrent monitoring approaches.\nSection~\\ref{sec:method} details our iterative contamination protocol, signal definitions, and evaluation framework.\nSection~\\ref{sec:results} presents signal--severity correlations, within-generation early warning analysis, and the method-dependent signal taxonomy.\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses practical monitoring recommendations and limitations.\nSection~\\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes.\n",

    "originalFile": "% Conclusion section — collapse_severity_monitor\n% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe presented a systematic empirical study of training-time signals as model collapse severity monitors.\nAcross three experimental configurations spanning two model scales (0.5B, 1.5B), two fine-tuning methods (QLoRA, full fine-tuning), and six contamination fractions on WikiText-2, we established three findings.\n\nFirst, spectral and output signals reliably track collapse severity across configurations.\nSpectral entropy achieves the largest and most reproducible effect size ($d = 33.48$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$ across seeds), followed by evaluation perplexity ($d = 20.82$) and stable rank ($d = 9.43$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$).\nSix of seven monitored signals maintain consistent direction of change regardless of training method.\n\nSecond, stable rank provides within-generation early warning, achieving $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.94$ at 10\\% training progress ($0.984$ pooled) and 34.4\\% deviation from baseline at 3\\% of training.\nTrain loss provides the second-strongest within-generation signal (pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$), though its decrease reflects successful fitting of corrupted data rather than improved model quality.\nHeld-out evaluation perplexity (pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.533$) and token entropy (pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$) fail at within-generation detection---the most commonly relied-upon quality metric is no better than chance at the most actionable task.\n\nThird, signal informativeness depends on the fine-tuning method and model scale, motivating a three-tier monitoring strategy: stable rank for within-generation alarms, evaluation perplexity for cross-generation tracking, and spectral entropy for severity discrimination.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nValidating these findings at larger scales ($\\geq$7B) and across diverse domains (cross-domain and 3B-scale experiments are ongoing) would establish generalizability.\nTranslating early warning signals into automated intervention triggers---adaptive learning rate reduction, selective data filtering, or training rollback when spectral deviation exceeds calibrated thresholds---would close the loop from detection to mitigation.\n",

    "newString": "We evaluate five signal families---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (train loss, evaluation perplexity, token entropy), diversity (distinct-$n$, self-BLEU), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---across three experimental configurations",
    "originalFile": "% Introduction — collapse_severity_monitor\n% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nAs LLM-generated text pervades web-scraped training corpora, practitioners increasingly fine-tune models on data shaped by prior model generations.\nThis recursive dynamic creates self-consuming loops that cause \\emph{model collapse}---progressive loss of distribution tails, contraction of output diversity, and degradation of acquired knowledge~\\citep{Shumailov2024,Alemohammad2024}.\nThe problem compounds: even trace synthetic contamination induces collapse at sufficient depth~\\citep{Dohmatob2025}, with errors accumulating across iterations at accelerating rates~\\citep{Dohmatob2024_tails,Fu2024_icml}.\nYet the metric practitioners most commonly rely on for quality assessment---held-out evaluation perplexity---fails to detect this degradation within a run, achieving pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.533$ for within-generation collapse detection (indistinguishable from random chance).\nTrain loss does change under collapse (pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$), but its decrease is interpretively ambiguous: the model fits lower-entropy synthetic data more easily, which looks like healthy convergence.\nA practitioner watching only perplexity or interpreting train loss at face value would have no reliable warning that collapse is underway.\n\nExisting work addresses model collapse from two directions.\nOn the theoretical side, \\citet{Shumailov2024} established the phenomenon, \\citet{Dohmatob2025} proved that any nonzero synthetic fraction eventually induces strong collapse, and \\citet{Dohmatob2024_tails} showed that collapse alters neural scaling laws.\nOn the mitigation side, data accumulation~\\citep{Gerstgrasser2024}, loss modification~\\citep{Zibakhsh2025}, and verification-based filtering~\\citep{Feng2025} each reduce collapse pressure under specific conditions.\nMissing from this landscape is a systematic analysis of which \\emph{training-time signals} best track collapse severity, when they provide early warning, and where they saturate or actively mislead.\n\\citet{Schaeffer2025} highlight that even the definition of collapse varies across studies, further motivating signal-level analysis grounded in controlled experiments.\nThe two closest concurrent works---SIGMA~\\citep{Gu2026}, which monitors output embedding spectra, and the topological index of \\citet{Kalinowski2026}---each address part of this gap but carry limitations: SIGMA requires a clean reference distribution and operates post-hoc; topological monitoring incurs higher computational cost.\nNeither provides a cross-family comparison of signal operating ranges or quantitative within-generation early warning thresholds.\n\nWe conduct a systematic empirical study of training-time signals as collapse severity monitors.\nWe evaluate four signal families---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (train loss, evaluation perplexity, token entropy), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---across three experimental configurations spanning two model scales (0.5B, 1.5B), two fine-tuning methods (QLoRA and full fine-tuning), and six contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0\\}$) on WikiText-2.\nOur central finding is that different signal families have complementary operating ranges, and the same signal's utility can flip from informative to misleading depending on the training method.\n\nOur contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Signal--severity correlation.}\n    Spectral and output signals track collapse across contamination levels.\n    Spectral entropy achieves the largest effect size (Cohen's $d = 33.48$, coefficient of variation $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$ across seeds), followed by evaluation perplexity ($d = 20.82$) and stable rank ($d = 9.43$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$).\n    Validated across QLoRA and full fine-tuning, ten of eleven monitored signals maintain consistent direction of change regardless of training method.\n    The exception---gradient norm, which is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning---demonstrates that method-dependence is a first-order concern for monitoring pipeline design.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Within-generation early warning and negative results.}\n    Stable rank achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.94$ at 10\\% training progress ($0.984$ pooled) for binary collapse detection, with 34.4\\% deviation from baseline at 3\\% of training.\n    Train loss provides the second-strongest within-generation signal (pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$), though its decrease reflects successful fitting of corrupted data rather than improved model quality.\n    Held-out evaluation perplexity achieves pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.533$ for within-generation detection---the most commonly relied-upon quality metric fails at the most actionable task---and token entropy achieves pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$, providing no reliable discrimination despite a large initial deviation.\n    Evaluation perplexity's failure has a structural explanation: it accumulates degradation through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single generation's training.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Signal taxonomy with method-dependent operating ranges.}\n    Signal informativeness depends on the fine-tuning method and model scale.\n    Gradient norm is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning.\n    HellaSwag accuracy is insensitive at 0.5B scale (relative change ${<}3\\%$, $N{=}1$).\n    Distinct-1 is non-monotonic, reversing direction between moderate and full contamination.\n    Full fine-tuning produces sharper collapse onset ($15.3\\times$ evaluation perplexity ratio at generation~1) than QLoRA ($8.7\\times$) but lower terminal severity.\n    These findings motivate a three-tier recommendation: stable rank for within-generation alarms, evaluation perplexity for cross-generation tracking, and spectral entropy for severity discrimination.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:related_work} surveys collapse theory, spectral analysis of neural networks, and concurrent monitoring approaches.\nSection~\\ref{sec:method} details our iterative contamination protocol, signal definitions, and evaluation framework.\nSection~\\ref{sec:results} presents signal--severity correlations, within-generation early warning analysis, and the method-dependent signal taxonomy.\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses practical monitoring recommendations and limitations.\nSection~\\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes.\n",

generated continuations per checkpoint.\nDistinct-$n$ captures lexical diversity; self-BLEU captures inter-sample repetition.\n```\n\n**Experiments §4.1 cross-method (修改后):**\n```latex\nTen of eleven monitored signals maintain consistent direction of change under both QLoRA and full fine-tuning (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}): the sole exception is gradient norm, which exhibits non-monotonic behavior under QLoRA but monotonic decrease under full fine-tuning (Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n```\n\n**Abstract (修改后，α 记法 + families):**\n```latex\n...five families of training-time signals---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (train loss, evaluation perplexity, token entropy), diversity (distinct-$n$, self-BLEU), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---as collapse severity monitors across three experimental configurations spanning two model scales (0.5B, 1.5B), two fine-tuning methods (QLoRA, full fine-tuning), and six contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0\\}$) on WikiText-2.\n```",
  "project": "collapse_severity_monitor"
}
````

  "sessionId": "66d59675-c6ab-4acf-8311-b61b563a3886",
  "content": "[Director 指令] [Full Draft] 提前启动论文写作，目标 EMNLP 2026 via ARR，DDL 5/25（剩 9 天）。\n\nCore narrative: 系统性实证分析 training dynamic signals 作为 model collapse severity monitors。Analysis paper，不是提出新方法——我们做的是首个 multi-signal, multi-scale, cross-domain 的 collapse monitoring 实证研究。\n\nCore Claims:\n- C1: 信号泛化性——14 个 training signals 在不同 model scale (0.5B/1.5B/3B)、不同 domain (WikiText/PubMed)、不同训练方法 (QLoRA/FullFT) 下都能 monitor collapse severity\n- C2: 早期预警——within-generation 阶段（~10% training progress）信号偏离即可预测 end-of-generation collapse\n- C3: 信号分类学——不同信号族（spectral/output-diversity/downstream）在不同 severity 级别有不同的灵敏度和适用范围\n\n已有数据（可直接写入论文）：\n- MVP (0.5B × WikiText × 3 fractions × 1 seed × 5 gens)：全部完成，gate check 3/3 PASS\n- plan_003 (FullFT vs QLoRA 0.5B)：完成，collapse dynamics 一致但 onset 更快\n- plan_006 Signal Taxonomy (preliminary, N=3 seeds)：spectral_entropy d=33.48 最稳定，分层结果有\n- plan_008 Self-Recovery (preliminary, N=3 seeds)：50% fraction recovery 100% prevalence, RR=0.52\n- plan_mvp_003 Within-Gen Early Warning：step 50 (3% progress) stable_rank 偏离 34.4%\n\n进行中数据（5/17-19 陆续到位）：\n- plan_001: 1.5B × WikiText × 5 seeds × 6 fractions（32%, ETA 5/19）\n- plan_002: 1.5B × PubMed × 3 seeds × 6 fractions（22%, 进行中）\n- plan_010: 3B spot-check（ETA 5/16 晚）\n\n写作策略：\n1. 先写不依赖全量数据的部分：Abstract (draft), Introduction, Related Work, Methods (signal definitions + experimental setup), Experimental Design\n2. Results 用 MVP + plan_003 + plan_006/008 preliminary 先填框架，标注 [PENDING: full data] 占位\n3. 5/17 plan_010 完成后 patch 3B 数据\n4. 5/19 plan_001 完成后全量 patch\n\n引用需要验证。特别注意：\n- Shumailov et al. 2024 (model collapse original)\n- Gerstgrasser et al. 2024 (data mixing / self-recovery context)\n- SIGMA 相关工作（spectral metrics for model quality）\n- Dohmatob et al. 2024 (scaling laws for collapse)"
}

_source line 2917 · 2026-05-16T02:46:33.573Z_

[Director 指令] [Full Draft] 提前启动论文写作，目标 EMNLP 2026 via ARR，DDL 5/25（剩 9 天）。

Core narrative: 系统性实证分析 training dynamic signals 作为 model collapse severity monitors。Analysis paper，不是提出新方法——我们做的是首个 multi-signal, multi-scale, cross-domain 的 collapse monitoring 实证研究。

Core Claims:
- C1: 信号泛化性——14 个 training signals 在不同 model scale (0.5B/1.5B/3B)、不同 domain (WikiText/PubMed)、不同训练方法 (QLoRA/FullFT) 下都能 monitor collapse severity
- C2: 早期预警——within-generation 阶段（~10% training progress）信号偏离即可预测 end-of-generation collapse
- C3: 信号分类学——不同信号族（spectral/output-diversity/downstream）在不同 severity 级别有不同的灵敏度和适用范围

已有数据（可直接写入论文）：
- MVP (0.5B × WikiText × 3 fractions × 1 seed × 5 gens)：全部完成，gate check 3/3 PASS
- plan_003 (FullFT vs QLoRA 0.5B)：完成，collapse dynamics 一致但 onset 更快
- plan_006 Signal Taxonomy (preliminary, N=3 seeds)：spectral_entropy d=33.48 最稳定，分层结果有
- plan_008 Self-Recovery (preliminary, N=3 seeds)：50% fraction recovery 100% prevalence, RR=0.52
- plan_mvp_003 Within-Gen Early Warning：step 50 (3% progress) stable_rank 偏离 34.4%

进行中数据（5/17-19 陆续到位）：
- plan_001: 1.5B × WikiText × 5 seeds × 6 fractions（32%, ETA 5/19）
- plan_002: 1.5B × PubMed × 3 seeds × 6 fractions（22%, 进行中）
- plan_010: 3B spot-check（ETA 5/16 晚）

写作策略：
1. 先写不依赖全量数据的部分：Abstract (draft), Introduction, Related Work, Methods (signal definitions + experimental setup), Experimental Design
2. Results 用 MVP + plan_003 + plan_006/008 preliminary 先填框架，标注 [PENDING: full data] 占位
3. 5/17 plan_010 完成后 patch 3B 数据
4. 5/19 plan_001 完成后全量 patch

引用需要验证。特别注意：
- Shumailov et al. 2024 (model collapse original)
- Gerstgrasser et al. 2024 (data mixing / self-recovery context)
- SIGMA 相关工作（spectral metrics for model quality）
- Dohmatob et al. 2024 (scaling laws for collapse)

[Director 指令] [Full Draft] 写一篇 EMNLP 2026 via ARR 论文，DDL 5月25日（剩 9 天）。

**Core Claim**: Training dynamic signals (spectral entropy, stable rank, perplexity, token entropy) serve as real-time severity monitors for model collapse in iterative fine-tuning, enabling early warning before catastrophic performance degradation.

**三个子 Claim**:
1. Signal-severity correlation: 信号与 collapse 严重度（contamination fraction）单调相关，跨模型规模(0.5B/1.5B/3B)、跨域(WikiText/PubMed)、跨训练方式(QLoRA/FullFT)泛化
2. Within-generation early warning: 训练过程中 10% progress 即可检测到 collapse 信号偏离
3. Signal taxonomy: 不同信号家族在不同 severity regime 有不同灵敏度，形成互补的监控矩阵（spectral_entropy 最稳定 CV=0.0015，stable_rank 对极端 collapse 敏感但 variance 高）

**已有实验数据**（可直接用于写作）:
- MVP 完成：0.5B × WikiText × 3 fractions × 1 seed ✓ (gate check 3/3 PASS)
- plan003 FullFT 完成：0.5B × WikiText × 3 fractions × full fine-tuning ✓
- plan006 Signal Taxonomy 初步完成：N=3 seeds, 14 signals ranked by Cohen's d ✓
- plan008 Self-Recovery 初步完成：50% fraction recovery prevalence 100%, cross-seed consistent ✓
- plan010 3B spot-check：frac=0.0 完成，frac=0.5 进行中（ETA ~15:00Z 今天）

**进行中实验**（数据陆续到位，需要 patch）:
- plan001: 1.5B × WikiText × 6 fractions × 5 seeds（32%，ETA 5/19）
- plan002: 1.5B × PubMed × 3 seeds（67%，ETA ~5/16-17）
- plan009: SIGMA head-to-head（被动收集中，随 plan001 完成）

**写作策略**:
1. 先写 structure/methods/related work/experimental setup（不依赖全量数据）
2. Results 用 MVP + plan003 + plan006/008 preliminary 数据先出初稿
3. plan001/002/010 数据到位后 Patch 更新 results tables/figures
4. 引用需要验证

**关键论文定位**: Analysis paper（不是 method paper）。我们不提新方法，而是系统性分析现有 training dynamics signals 作为 collapse severity monitor 的有效性。Related work 要充分对比 SIGMA (Dohmatob et al.)、model collapse 理论工作（Shumailov et al. 2024, Gerstgrasser et al. 2024）。

**Self-recovery 定位**: 观察性发现，与 data mixing literature 一致（Gerstgrasser et al. 2024），不作为 novel discovery 宣称。

99:The generation~0 to generation~1 evaluation perplexity ratio is $15.3\times$ under full FT versus $8.7\times$ under QLoRA.
100:Post-onset, the trajectory is non-monotonic: evaluation perplexity \emph{decreases} from $294.0$ at generation~1 to $288.5$ at generation~2 before rising through generations~3--4 to a terminal value of $326.19$.
102:The overall generation~1 to generation~4 ratio remains $1.11\times$, compared to continued monotonic growth under QLoRA, and terminal severity is lower: $\mathrm{PPL}_\text{eval} = 326.19$ versus QLoRA's $839.2$ ($0.39\times$).
103:This pattern---fast onset, partial dip, then resumed rise at lower terminal severity---is consistent with full parameter access enabling rapid adaptation to synthetic data characteristics.

26:Its $\mathrm{CV}$ of 0.022 is an order of magnitude larger than spectral entropy's 0.0015.
118:    \item \textbf{Cross-generation severity discrimination:} spectral entropy ($d = 33.48$, $\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$).

[Director 指令] [Patch] 新增 cross-domain recovery 发现，纳入 Discussion 或 §4.3。

**数据**（PubMed vs WikiText, frac=50%, N=2 seeds）：
- PPL recovery ratio: 0.41 (PubMed) vs 0.53 (WikiText)
- Stable rank RR: 0.18 vs 0.32
- Spectral entropy RR: 0.15 vs 0.32
- Recovery prevalence: 80% (PubMed) vs 100% (WikiText)

**解读**：专业领域文本分布更窄，合成数据 corruption 更难被真实数据覆盖，导致 recovery 系统性更弱。这强化了 monitoring 在 domain-specific fine-tuning 场景中的实际价值。

**定位**：作为 Claim 3 的 nuance 或 Discussion 中的 practical implication。不升格为独立 claim（N=2 seeds 还不够）。plan002 全部完成后（ETA ~明天）会有 N=3 seeds 数据再做一次 patch 更新统计量。

36	\subsection{Partial Recovery at Moderate Contamination}
37	\label{sec:recovery}

  "new_string": "The partial recovery at moderate contamination documented in Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy} is consistent with data accumulation effects~\\citep{Gerstgrasser2024}: the retained real data fraction attenuates collapse pressure.\nThe practical consequence is that monitoring thresholds should be calibrated to the expected contamination level rather than set as binary decisions.\n\nPreliminary cross-domain results ($N = 2$ seeds, PubMed abstracts at $\\alpha = 0.5$) indicate that recovery is systematically weaker in specialized domains: evaluation perplexity recovery ratio is $0.41$ (PubMed) versus $0.53$ (WikiText), and spectral signal recovery ratios are $0.15$--$0.18$ (PubMed) versus $0.32$ (WikiText), with recovery prevalence of $80\\%$ versus $100\\%$.\nThis attenuation is consistent with narrower domain-specific distributions being harder to recover through real data mixing, reinforcing the practical value of monitoring in domain-specific fine-tuning scenarios where collapse pressure may be less forgiving."

    "oldString": "The partial recovery at moderate contamination documented in Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy} is consistent with data accumulation effects~\\citep{Gerstgrasser2024}: the retained real data fraction attenuates collapse pressure.\nThe practical consequence is that monitoring thresholds should be calibrated to the expected contamination level rather than set as binary decisions.",
    "newString": "The partial recovery at moderate contamination documented in Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy} is consistent with data accumulation effects~\\citep{Gerstgrasser2024}: the retained real data fraction attenuates collapse pressure.\nThe practical consequence is that monitoring thresholds should be calibrated to the expected contamination level rather than set as binary decisions.\n\nPreliminary cross-domain results ($N = 2$ seeds, PubMed abstracts at $\\alpha = 0.5$) indicate that recovery is systematically weaker in specialized domains: evaluation perplexity recovery ratio is $0.41$ (PubMed) versus $0.53$ (WikiText), and spectral signal recovery ratios are $0.15$--$0.18$ (PubMed) versus $0.32$ (WikiText), with recovery prevalence of $80\\%$ versus $100\\%$.\nThis attenuation is consistent with narrower domain-specific distributions being harder to recover through real data mixing, reinforcing the practical value of monitoring in domain-specific fine-tuning scenarios where collapse pressure may be less forgiving.",
    "originalFile": "% Discussion section — collapse_severity_monitor\n% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B, heatmap_data.json\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Practical Three-Tier Monitoring Recommendation}\n\\label{sec:three_tier}\n\nNo single signal suffices for collapse monitoring across all operating regimes.\nOur results motivate a three-tier strategy that assigns each signal family to the task where it excels:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep]\n    \\item \\textbf{Within-generation alarm} $\\to$ stable rank.\n    Stable rank achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.94$ at 10\\% training progress ($0.984$ pooled), with $\\Delta_\\mathrm{SR} = 34.4\\%$ at step~50 (3\\% of training).\n    This structural sensitivity---detecting energy dispersal across representation dimensions before output-level metrics respond---provides the earliest actionable warning.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-generation severity tracking} $\\to$ evaluation perplexity.\n    With a large absolute effect size ($d = 20.82$) and monotonic growth across generations, evaluation perplexity provides an interpretable severity scale that maps directly to language modeling quality on held-out text.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Severity discrimination} $\\to$ spectral entropy.\n    The largest effect size ($d = 33.48$) and lowest coefficient of variation ($\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$) across seeds make spectral entropy the most reproducible discriminator between contamination levels.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\noindent\nThis assignment reflects a core finding: the signal that best detects collapse onset (stable rank, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$, seed-sensitive) differs from the one that most reproducibly quantifies its severity (spectral entropy).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Perplexity: Within-Generation Failure Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:ppl_failure}\n\nEvaluation perplexity achieves only overall $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.533$---indistinguishable from chance---despite being the primary severity indicator across generations.\nAs discussed in Section~\\ref{sec:early_warning_results}, this failure is structural: collapse pressure enters only at the next generation, so evaluation perplexity accumulates degradation through the iterative loop, not within a single run.\nTrain loss ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$ at completion) is the second-strongest within-generation signal, but its trajectory reveals early-training direction reversal: AUROC starts at $0.24$ at 10\\% progress (below chance) because the collapsing model's loss is initially \\emph{lower}, recovering only as cumulative degradation raises loss in the second half of training (Section~\\ref{sec:early_warning_results}).\nFor practitioners, the implication is that evaluation perplexity is a lagging indicator of collapse, and train loss changes require spectral cross-validation to interpret correctly.\n\n\\subsection{Partial Recovery at Moderate Contamination}\n\\label{sec:recovery}\n\nThe partial recovery at moderate contamination documented in Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy} is consistent with data accumulation effects~\\citep{Gerstgrasser2024}: the retained real data fraction attenuates collapse pressure.\nThe practical consequence is that monitoring thresholds should be calibrated to the expected contamination level rather than set as binary decisions.\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Concurrent Monitoring Approaches}\n\\label{sec:sigma_comparison}\n\nThe signals evaluated here incur near-zero marginal cost: they are computed from quantities already available during standard training and require no clean reference distribution.\nSIGMA~\\citep{Gu2026} operates post-hoc on the output embedding Gram matrix and requires a clean reference distribution for comparison.\n\\citet{Kalinowski2026} uses persistent homology, incurring higher computational cost from a different signal family entirely.\nNeither provides the systematic comparison of signal families, operating range characterization across contamination fractions, or within-generation early warning documented here.\nThese approaches are complementary: SIGMA and topological methods may capture aspects of collapse that spectral covariance signals miss, while our signals offer the advantage of online, reference-free monitoring integrated into the training loop.\n\n\\subsection{Terminal Spectral Convergence}\n\\label{sec:terminal_convergence}\n\nTerminal stable rank values at generation~4, $\\alpha = 1.0$ are strikingly similar across methods: $\\mathrm{SR} = 153.87$ (full FT) versus $155.64$ (QLoRA), a 1.2\\% difference despite divergent output-level severity.\nThis observation rests on $N = 1$ seed per method and cannot be distinguished from coincidence; see Appendix~\\ref{app:terminal_convergence} for discussion.\n",

    "oldString": "All reported results use Qwen2.5-0.5B and 1.5B models.\nScale validation at 3B and cross-domain validation on PubMed are ongoing but not yet complete.",
    "newString": "All reported results use Qwen2.5-0.5B and 1.5B models.\nPreliminary 3B baseline results confirm signal stability ($<3\\%$ variation across generations at $\\alpha = 0$), but severity gradient validation at 3B and cross-domain validation on PubMed are ongoing.",
    "originalFile": "% Limitations section — collapse_severity_monitor\n\nOur study has several limitations that bound the scope of our conclusions.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale.}\nAll reported results use Qwen2.5-0.5B and 1.5B models.\nScale validation at 3B and cross-domain validation on PubMed are ongoing but not yet complete.\nWhether signal--severity relationships and operating ranges transfer to models at scales of practical deployment ($\\geq$7B) remains unvalidated.\nStable rank baselines are known to be scale-dependent~\\citep{Jha2025}, and threshold calibration may require per-scale adjustment.\n\n\\paragraph{Seed count and statistical power.}\nCross-generation effect sizes are computed from $N = 3$ seeds (C2 configuration, interim 3 of 5 planned seeds); within-generation AUROC results use $N = 1$ seed at 0.5B (C1).\nThe full fine-tuning comparison (C4) uses $N = 1$ seed.\nThese sample sizes are insufficient for rank-based tests (minimum $p = 0.25$ for Wilcoxon with $N = 3$), and we rely on bootstrap confidence intervals.\nStable rank's coefficient of variation ($\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$) is an order of magnitude larger than spectral entropy's ($0.0015$), and its effect size dropped from $d = 61.7$ to $d = 9.43$ when a third seed was added---underscoring that our ranking of signals by sensitivity may shift with more seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain coverage.}\nAll reported results use WikiText-2; cross-domain validation on PubMed is ongoing.\nWhether our signal taxonomy generalizes to biomedical text, code, dialogue, multilingual text, or instruction-following data is unknown.\n\n\\paragraph{Repetition confound.}\nAt $\\alpha = 1.0$, the 1{,}500 unique synthetic chunks are upsampled to 4{,}916, introducing repetition (${\\sim}3.3\\times$ per epoch) that may contribute to observed training dynamics through memorization rather than synthetic data quality degradation alone.\nHowever, this confound is mitigated at moderate contamination: at $\\alpha = 0.5$, the corpus contains 2{,}458 real and 1{,}500 synthetic chunks with minimal repetition, yet the dose-response trend and partial recovery conclusions (Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy}) remain intact.\nThe $\\alpha = 0$ control undergoes the identical training procedure without collapse, ruling out the training process itself as a confound.\n\n\\paragraph{Collapse source.}\nOur iterative retraining protocol simulates a specific collapse mechanism---self-consuming loops with controlled contamination fractions.\nReal-world collapse may involve mixed provenance (multiple model generations), distribution shift between generations, or partial synthetic contamination at unknown fractions.\nSignal behavior under these conditions may differ from our controlled setting.\n\n\\paragraph{Fine-tuning methods.}\nWe compare QLoRA and full fine-tuning.\nOther parameter-efficient methods (LoRA without quantization, adapters, prefix tuning) may interact differently with spectral signals due to different parameterization constraints.\n\n\\paragraph{Causal claims.}\nWe establish correlations between signals and collapse severity, not causal mechanisms.\nThe spectral entropy increase under collapse reflects a change in representation structure, but we do not prove that this change causes (rather than accompanies) output degradation.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01L3UC1gpxqo5i6UNqsHhzb7`</summary>

```text
22	We conduct a systematic empirical study of training-time signals as collapse severity monitors.
23	We evaluate five signal families---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (train loss, evaluation perplexity, token entropy), diversity (distinct-$n$, self-BLEU), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---across three experimental configurations spanning two model scales (0.5B, 1.5B), two fine-tuning methods (QLoRA and full fine-tuning), and six contamination fractions ($\alpha \in \{0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0\}$) on WikiText-2 (Figure~\ref{fig:framework}).
24	Our central finding is that different signal families have complementary operating ranges, and the same signal's utility can flip from informative to misleading depending on the training method.
```

125	\noindent
126	Effect sizes in Section~\ref{sec:correlation} are from C2 (1.5B, $N=3$ interim seeds); within-generation AUROC values are from C1 (0.5B, $N=1$ seed); method-dependent operating ranges are from C1 vs C4 (0.5B, $N=1$ seed each).

129	Validation across larger seed counts and additional domains is required before these operating ranges can be considered robust.

_source line 3034 · 2026-05-16T06:25:19.241Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director is coordinating iterative refinement (Step 5) and Patch-mode updates for an EMNLP 2026 paper "Training Dynamic Signals as Model Collapse Severity Monitors" submitted via ACL Rolling Review. The paper systematically evaluates training-time signals (spectral, output, diversity, gradient, downstream) as collapse severity monitors. The workflow is: process Reviewer feedback → compile → submit next Reviewer round → report to Director. The paper went through 4 Reviewer rounds (Round 1→4), achieving convergence at Overall 6/10. After convergence, the Director locked the baseline and switched to Patch mode for incremental data updates (plan010 3B, plan002 PubMed, plan001 5-seed). The most recent task is patching 3B baseline data into the paper while maintaining the 8-page ACL main content limit.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Model collapse from iterative synthetic data training (self-consuming loops)
   - 5 signal families (11 signals): spectral (stable_rank, spectral_entropy), output (train_loss, eval_perplexity, token_entropy), diversity (distinct-1/2/3, self-BLEU), gradient (gradient_norm), downstream (HellaSwag)
   - Cohen's d as PRIMARY sensitivity measure (valid with N≥2 seeds from C2 config)
   - AUROC: pooled vs progress-point distinction for within-generation detection
   - Within-gen detection hierarchy: stable_rank (pooled 0.984) > train_loss (0.923) > eval_perplexity (0.533)
   - 3 completed experimental configs: C1 (0.5B/QLoRA/1-seed), C2 (1.5B/QLoRA/3-of-5-seeds), C4 (0.5B/FullFT/1-seed)
   - 2 ongoing: C3 (PubMed), C5 (3B)
   - ACL Rolling Review 8-page main content limit
   - Paper Agent workflow: edit_paper_section for provenance, paper_agent_post_to_director for Director communication, paper_agent_request_review for Reviewer submission
   - Cross-domain recovery: PubMed shows weaker recovery than WikiText (specialized domain distributions harder to recover)
   - Repetition confound at α=1.0: 1,500 chunks upsampled to 4,916 (~3.3× repetition)

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **paper/sections/abstract.tex**
     - Central scope declaration for the paper; must be consistent with all other sections
     - Changes: "four families" → "five families" + added "diversity (distinct-$n$, self-BLEU)"; α notation changed to list all 6 values explicitly: `$\alpha \in \{0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0\}

; scope: "three experimental configurations spanning two model scales (0.5B, 1.5B)...on WikiText-2"

   - **paper/sections/introduction.tex**
     - Contains contributions list and scope claims; has new `fig:framework` reference added by another session
     - Changes: "four signal families" → "five signal families" + diversity; "six of seven" → "ten of eleven"; scope matching abstract; line 23 now has `(Figure~\ref{fig:framework})` — this is a NEW undefined reference not from my edits

   - **paper/sections/experiments.tex** (most heavily edited)
     - Core results section with 3 subsections: §4.1 Signal-Severity Correlation, §4.2 Within-Gen Early Warning, §4.3 Signal Taxonomy
     - Changes made:
       - Line 19: distinct-1 parenthetical split into separate sentence; "effective $N=2$ seeds; one seed excluded due to missing diversity metrics at $\alpha{=}0.5$"
       - Line 31: "Six of seven" → "Ten of eleven monitored signals maintain consistent direction... the sole exception is gradient norm"
       - Line 93: "$17\times$" → "$17\times$ (generation~4, $\alpha = 1.0$, C4)"
       - Line 101 (now ~102): "Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy}" self-reference → "(below)"
       - Lines 110: "weight-space" → "representation-space" (done in prior session)
       - End of §4.3 (line ~127): Added 3B baseline note, currently condensed to: "Preliminary 3B baseline results (C5, $\alpha = 0$) confirm signal stability ($<3\%$ variation across generations), consistent with 0.5B and 1.5B; severity validation ($\alpha > 0$) at 3B, additional domains, and larger seed counts is ongoing."

   - **paper/sections/method.tex**
     - Defines experimental protocol, signal definitions, and configurations
     - Changes: Line 44: "four families (seven signals)" → "five families (eleven signals)"; Added new paragraph "Diversity and repetition signals" defining distinct-n and self-BLEU after the Downstream signal paragraph; Line 24: Added upsampling sentence after Mix equation: "When the number of synthetic chunks is fewer than $|\mathcal{D}_\text{real}|$, we upsample with replacement to maintain a fixed training set size of $|\mathcal{D}_\text{real}|$ across all configurations."

   - **paper/sections/discussion.tex**
     - Contains practical recommendations, failure analysis, recovery discussion, competitor comparison
     - Changes: §5.4 removed "and provide per-layer granularity"; "weight-space" → "representation-space"; §5.3 added cross-domain recovery paragraph: "Preliminary cross-domain results ($N = 2$ seeds, PubMed abstracts at $\alpha = 0.5$) indicate that recovery is systematically weaker in specialized domains: evaluation perplexity recovery ratio is $0.41$ (PubMed) versus $0.53$ (WikiText), and spectral signal recovery ratios are $0.15$--$0.18$ (PubMed) versus $0.32$ (WikiText), with recovery prevalence of $80\%$ versus $100\%$."

   - **paper/sections/related_work.tex**
     - Last sentence changed from "...online monitoring, spectral/entropic training-dynamic signals, per-layer granularity, and validation..." to "...online monitoring, spectral/entropic training-dynamic signals, and validation..."

   - **paper/sections/conclusion.tex**
     - Changes: "Six of seven" → "Ten of eleven"; scope matching abstract/intro

   - **paper/sections/limitations.tex**
     - Changes: Added "Repetition confound" paragraph (3 sentences covering confound + mitigating factors); Scale paragraph updated: "Preliminary 3B baseline results confirm signal stability ($<3\%$ variation across generations at $\alpha = 0$), but severity gradient validation at 3B and cross-domain validation on PubMed are ongoing."

4. Errors and Fixes:
   - **Page count exceeded 8 pages**: After adding 3B baseline text (2 sentences) + prior cumulative patches, main content grew to 9 pages (Limitations starts page 10). Fix: Condensed 3B text from 3 sentences to 1 compound sentence. Recompilation pending.
   - **`fig:framework` undefined reference**: New reference appeared on page 2 of introduction.tex line 23, added by another session. Not my edit — pre-existing placeholder like other figure refs.
   - **paper_compile_and_check reports "FAIL"**: Tool always reports failure due to pre-existing undefined figure/table references (fig:severity_curves, fig:signal_heatmap, fig:roc_curves, etc.) and total page count exceeding max_pages (which includes appendix/refs). The actual PDF is generated successfully. Workaround: verify main content pages separately using pdftotext.
   - **Stale Director instructions**: Two Full Draft instructions sent after Step 5 convergence. Flagged both as contradictory to prior "wait for Patch" instruction. Director confirmed stale.
   - **System reminders about post_to_director**: Multiple "[系统提醒]" messages saying I didn't call post_to_director. In each case I had already called it or there was nothing new to report. I responded with "No response requested."

5. Problem Solving:
   - **Signal set mismatch (7 vs 11)**: Discovered Method defined 7 signals but Results used 11 (distinct-1/2/3, self-BLEU missing from Method). Added "Diversity and repetition signals" paragraph to Method, updated all counts (4→5 families, 7→11 signals, 6/7→10/11 consistent).
   - **"effective N=2" explanation**: Checked registry; Director confirmed seed44's frac=50% distinct_1 data all null. Updated explanation text.
   - **"17×" verification**: Checked registry to confirm source: C4 fullft_100pct_gen4 PPL=326.19 / fullft_0pct_gen4 PPL=19.23 = 17.0×.
   - **Cross-domain recovery**: Added preliminary PubMed vs WikiText comparison to Discussion with appropriate hedging (N=2, "preliminary").
   - **Page budget management**: Ongoing challenge — each patch adds text but 8-page limit is hard. Condensing text to fit.

6. All User Messages:
   - "Continue from where you left off." (multiple times — context recovery)
   - "[系统提醒] 你刚才完成了 [Director 指令] 但没有调用 main_agent_post_to_director 汇报结果" (multiple system reminders)
   - "[Director 指令] CRITICAL 2 已解决，补充信息：训练集大小：代码确认 α=1.0 时 1,500 synthetic chunks 上采样...不存在训练集缩小问题。需要在论文中补充的内容（2处）..."
   - "[Director 指令] CRITICAL 2 已有答案，请补充到论文中：Method §3.1 补充...Discussion 或 Limitations 补充..."
   - "[Director 指令] ⚠️ Reviewer Round 2 已经返回，Overall 仍然 4/10...CRITICAL — Scope 降级（4处）...WARNING — weight-space...WARNING — Per-layer..."
   - "[Director 指令] 不需要重新提交。Reviewer Round 2 已在审查中..."
   - "[Director 指令] Round 3 反馈，Overall 6/10，无 CRITICAL。WARNING 1 — 信号集不匹配...WARNING 2 — effective N=2...INFO 1-3..."
   - "[Director 指令] distinct-1 'effective N=2' 原因确认：seed44 的 frac=50% distinct_1 数据全部为 null...请修正为 'one seed excluded due to missing diversity metrics at α=0.5'"
   - "[Director 指令] Step 5 收敛确认，迭代结束。当前版本锁定为 baseline。下一阶段：等实验数据更新后做 Patch..."
   - "[Director 指令] [Full Draft] 提前启动论文写作..." (stale instruction)
   - "[Director 指令] [Full Draft] 写一篇 EMNLP 2026 via ARR 论文..." (stale instruction)
   - "[Director 指令] 确认：这是过期指令冲突，不是要重写。保持 Patch 模式。现在可以立即 Patch 的：plan003/plan006/plan008..."
   - "[Director 指令] [Patch] 新增 cross-domain recovery 发现，纳入 Discussion 或 §4.3。数据：PubMed vs WikiText RR..."
   - "[服务器重启后首条消息] [Director 指令] [Patch] plan010 3B frac=0.0 数据已完成，可以 patch 进论文。"
   - "CRITICAL: Respond with TEXT ONLY..." (summary request)

7. Pending Tasks:
   - **Recompile after 3B text condensation**: Need to verify main content is back to ≤8 pages after condensing the 3B baseline paragraph
   - **Report 3B patch to Director**: via paper_agent_post_to_director

        "content": "% Results section — collapse_severity_monitor\n% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B, heatmap_data.json\n\n\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around three claims.\nSection~\\ref{sec:correlation} establishes that spectral and output signals track collapse severity across configurations.\nSection~\\ref{sec:early_warning_results} shows that certain signals detect collapse within a single training run before evaluation perplexity diverges.\nSection~\\ref{sec:taxonomy} reveals that signal informativeness depends on the fine-tuning method, motivating a taxonomy of method-dependent operating ranges.\n\n\\subsection{Signal--Severity Correlation}\n\\label{sec:correlation}\n\nWe quantify signal sensitivity using Cohen's $d$ between the clean baseline ($\\alpha = 0$) and full contamination ($\\alpha = 1.0$) at generation~4, computed across three seeds from the C2 configuration (Qwen2.5-1.5B, WikiText-2; interim 3 of 5 planned seeds; Table~\\ref{tab:configs}).\nEffect sizes, rather than rank correlations, serve as the primary evidence because they convey the magnitude of signal separation, not just its ordering.\n\n\\paragraph{Effect sizes.}\nSpectral entropy achieves the largest and most reproducible effect size ($d = 33.48$, coefficient of variation across seeds $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$), followed by evaluation perplexity ($d = 20.82$), distinct-1 ($d = 14.60$, effective $N=2$ seeds; one seed excluded due to missing diversity computation in summary reconstruction), and stable rank ($d = 9.43$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$).\nDistinct-1's large endpoint separation coexists with non-monotonic intermediate behavior at 0.5B (Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\nDistinct-2 ($d = 7.59$) and distinct-3 ($d = -7.08$; negative because collapse increases trigram repetition) provide moderate separation.\nToken entropy, self-BLEU, gradient norm, and HellaSwag accuracy yield small effect sizes ($d < 5$), placing them in a second tier for cross-generation severity discrimination (Figure~\\ref{fig:severity_curves}).\n\nStable rank warrants a cautionary note on seed sensitivity.\nWith two seeds, $d = 61.7$; adding a third seed reduced this to $d = 9.43$---a rank change from 1st to 4th among monitored signals.\nIts $\\mathrm{CV}$ of 0.022 is an order of magnitude larger than spectral entropy's 0.0015.\nWe treat stable rank as informative but seed-sensitive at this sample size; $N = 3$ seeds are insufficient for a Wilcoxon test (minimum $p = 0.25$), and we rely on bootstrap confidence intervals for inference.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-method consistency.}\nTen of eleven monitored signals maintain consistent direction of change under both QLoRA and full fine-tuning (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}): the sole exception is gradient norm, which exhibits non-monotonic behavior under QLoRA but monotonic decrease under full fine-tuning (Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Supporting rank correlations.}\nSpearman correlations provide directional confirmation: spectral entropy maintains high $|\\rho|$ across generations.\nHowever, $|\\rho| = 1.0$ for stable rank across contamination fractions is trivially achieved with few contamination levels and should not be interpreted as strong evidence of monotonicity.\nWe therefore use Cohen's $d$ as the primary sensitivity measure throughout.\n\n\n\\subsection{Within-Generation Early Warning}\n\\label{sec:early_warning_results}\n\nCross-generation tracking (Section~\\ref{sec:correlation}) requires completing multiple generations to assess severity.\nA more actionable capability is detecting collapse \\emph{within} a single training run, enabling early stopping before degradation accumulates.\nWe evaluate this using the relative deviation $\\Delta_s$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:deviation}) and AUROC for binary collapse detection at each checkpoint.\n\n\\paragraph{Stable rank as early alarm.}\nStable rank achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.94$ at 10\\% training progress for distinguishing $\\alpha = 1.0$ from $\\alpha = 0$, reaching $0.984$ over the full run (Figure~\\ref{fig:roc_curves}; Table~\\ref{tab:within_gen_roc}).\nAt step~50 (3\\% of training), $\\Delta_\\mathrm{SR} = 34.4\\%$---far exceeding the 5\\% detection threshold defined in Section~\\ref{sec:evaluation}.\nStable rank's within-generation sensitivity reflects the structural nature of spectral signals: contamination alters the covariance eigenspectrum before output-level metrics respond, because energy dispersal across representation dimensions (Eq.~\\ref{eq:stable_rank}) is detectable even when the model's token-level predictions have not yet degraded.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Spectral and token entropy.}\nToken entropy deviates 24.5\\% at step~50 but achieves only $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$ (pooled)---near random chance, indicating that despite its large initial deviation, token entropy provides no reliable within-generation discrimination.\nSpectral entropy achieves pooled $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.773$, providing moderate within-generation discrimination but still substantially below stable rank's pooled $0.984$.\n\n\\paragraph{Train loss: effective but interpretively ambiguous.}\nTrain loss achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$ at training completion, the second-strongest within-generation signal after stable rank.\nHowever, its AUROC trajectory reveals early-training direction reversal (Figure~\\ref{fig:roc_curves}b): $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.24$ at 10\\% progress, rising to $0.48$ at 20\\%, $0.85$ at 50\\%, and $0.923$ at completion.\nThe low early AUROC (${<}\\,0.5$) indicates that the collapsing run's training loss is initially \\emph{lower} than the clean baseline---the model fits the lower-entropy synthetic distribution more easily, a direct manifestation of the collapse mechanism.\nAs training progresses, cumulative degradation raises loss in the collapsed run, gradually restoring discriminability.\nTrain loss is therefore unreliable as an early collapse alarm (AUROC below chance for the first ${\\sim}20\\%$ of training), but effective at completion.\nPractitioners should cross-validate with spectral indicators such as stable rank, which achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.94$ from 10\\% progress onward.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation perplexity: no within-generation signal.}\nHeld-out evaluation perplexity achieves only $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.533$ across the full run---indistinguishable from random.\nThis failure has a structural explanation: within a single generation, the model trains on a fixed corpus $\\mathcal{D}_g$ and has not yet produced the synthetic data that will contaminate $\\mathcal{D}_{g+1}$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mixing}).\nEvaluation perplexity accumulates degradation \\emph{across} generations through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single run.\nConsequently, it does not diverge from baseline within one generation, even when collapse is underway.\n\nSpectral signals, by contrast, detect structural change---the dispersal of representational energy across dimensions---independent of whether the model's held-out predictions have degraded.\nThis yields a three-level hierarchy of within-generation detection capability (all pooled AUROC): stable rank ($0.984$) detects changes in representation-space structure, train loss ($0.923$) detects changes in training data distribution, and held-out evaluation perplexity ($0.533$) measures generalization quality---which does not diverge within a single generation.\nThe weakest link in within-generation monitoring is precisely the metric practitioners most commonly check at evaluation checkpoints.\n\n\n\n\\subsection{Signal Taxonomy: Method-Dependent Operating Ranges}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy}\n\nThe cross-method comparison (C1 vs C4; Table~\\ref{tab:configs}) reveals that the same signal can be informative under one training method and misleading under another.\n\n\\paragraph{Gradient norm: method-dependent.}\nUnder QLoRA, gradient norm changes non-monotonically with contamination: $+5.9\\%$ at $\\alpha = 0.5$ relative to baseline, then $-30.8\\%$ at $\\alpha = 1.0$.\nUnder full fine-tuning, gradient norm decreases monotonically ($-58.3\\%$ at $\\alpha = 1.0$), consistent with the model converging more easily to lower-entropy synthetic data.\nThe QLoRA non-monotonicity likely arises from the interaction between contaminated gradients and the rank-$r$ adapter subspace~\\citep{hu2022lora}: at moderate contamination, gradients from mixed real/synthetic data create conflicting update directions within the low-rank projection, inflating the norm; at full contamination, the uniform synthetic signal reduces this conflict~\\citep{dettmers2023qlora}.\nThis method-dependence means gradient norm cannot be used as a collapse indicator without knowing the training configuration (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}).\n\n\\paragraph{Downstream and diversity metrics: insensitive at 0.5B.}\nHellaSwag accuracy is insensitive under QLoRA (relative change $-1.15\\%$; $N=1$ seed at 0.5B, no effect size computable).\nUnder full fine-tuning, the $\\alpha = 0$ baseline is unavailable, preventing direct effect size computation; the available data show accuracy of $0.397$ ($\\alpha = 0.5$) and $0.372$ ($\\alpha = 1.0$), a $-6.3\\%$ relative change between these two conditions.\n\nDistinct-1 is non-monotonic under QLoRA: $+17.7\\%$ at $\\alpha = 0.5$ but $-12.5\\%$ at $\\alpha = 1.0$, reversing direction between moderate and full contamination.\nSelf-BLEU is insensitive to collapse even when evaluation perplexity increases $17\\times$ (generation~4, $\\alpha = 1.0$, C4).\nThis disconnect arises because nucleus sampling ($p = 0.95$) draws from the distribution tail, producing superficially diverse surface forms even as the model's probability mass concentrates on fewer modes.\nToken entropy captures this concentration (Section~\\ref{sec:correlation}), but $n$-gram diversity metrics do not.\nMonitoring pipelines that rely solely on output diversity metrics risk missing collapse entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Full fine-tuning collapse dynamics.}\nFull fine-tuning produces sharper onset but lower terminal severity than QLoRA (Figure~\\ref{fig:method_comparison}).\nThe generation~0 to generation~1 evaluation perplexity ratio is $15.3\\times$ under full FT versus $8.7\\times$ under QLoRA.\nPost-onset, the trajectory is non-monotonic: evaluation perplexity \\emph{decreases} from $294.0$ at generation~1 to $288.5$ at generation~2 before rising through generations~3--4 to a terminal value of $326.19$.\nThis generation~2 dip echoes the partial recovery observed at moderate contamination (below), suggesting that full parameter access allows transient re-adaptation even under full synthetic pressure.\nThe overall generation~1 to generation~4 ratio remains $1.11\\times$, compared to continued monotonic growth under QLoRA, and terminal severity is lower: $\\mathrm{PPL}_\\text{eval} = 326.19$ versus QLoRA's $839.2$ ($0.39\\times$).\nThis pattern---fast onset, partial dip, then resumed rise at lower terminal severity---is consistent with full parameter access enabling rapid adaptation to synthetic data characteristics.\n\nWe observe that terminal stable rank values are similar across methods: $\\mathrm{SR} = 153.87$ (full FT) versus $155.64$ (QLoRA) at generation~4, $\\alpha = 1.0$.\nThis comparison rests on $N = 1$ seed per method and cannot be distinguished from coincidence; we note it as an observation, not a finding.\n\n\\paragraph{Partial recovery at moderate contamination.}\nAt $\\alpha = 0.5$, signals show partial recovery across generations relative to the $\\alpha = 1.0$ trajectory.\nOutput signals (evaluation perplexity, token entropy) recover approximately 52\\% of the clean-to-collapsed gap, while representation-space signals (stable rank, spectral entropy) recover approximately 33\\%.\nThis pattern is consistent with data accumulation effects~\\citep{Gerstgrasser2024}: the retained real data fraction attenuates collapse pressure.\nRecovery ratios are computed within each signal family; cross-family prevalence comparisons are not meaningful because the signals have different scales and sensitivity profiles.\n\n\\paragraph{Recommended operating ranges.}\nBased on the evidence above, we propose signal-to-task assignments (Table~\\ref{tab:signal_taxonomy}):\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep]\n    \\item \\textbf{Within-generation alarm:} stable rank ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.94$ at 10\\% progress, $0.984$ pooled).\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-generation severity discrimination:} spectral entropy ($d = 33.48$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$).\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-generation tracking:} evaluation perplexity (large absolute effect, interpretable scale).\n    \\item \\textbf{Method-dependent:} gradient norm (monotonic under full FT only).\n    \\item \\textbf{Non-monotonic:} distinct-1 ($+17.7\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.5$, $-12.5\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ under QLoRA).\n    \\item \\textbf{Insensitive at 0.5B scale:} HellaSwag accuracy (relative change ${<}3\\%$, $N{=}1$).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent\nEffect sizes in Section~\\ref{sec:correlation} are from C2 (1.5B, $N=3$ interim seeds); within-generation AUROC values are from C1 (0.5B, $N=1$ seed); method-dependent operating ranges are from C1 vs C4 (0.5B, $N=1$ seed each).\nPreliminary 3B baseline results (C5, $\\alpha = 0$) confirm signal stability ($<3\\%$ variation across generations), consistent with 0.5B and 1.5B; severity validation ($\\alpha > 0$) at 3B, additional domains, and larger seed counts is ongoing.\n",
        "numLines": 128,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 128
      }
